I suppose I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your job or daily activities due to these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I’ve missed some application deadlines, and when I do get interviews, I don’t come across well. I get tangled up.\n\nI’ve also stopped volunteering for story-time unless someone else does the planning. So… yes, it’s getting in the way of me getting back into a classroom in any dependable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel short of breath, or experience palpitations, when these worries come up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that, no… not in any clear way. It’s more that I get caught up in the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your own health?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I’m 53, so things cross my mind now and then, but I wouldn’t say my health is what I’m usually worrying about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ve started avoiding things that would put me back in that role, I guess. Applications, interviews… even story-time, unless someone else handles the planning. It’s just hard to trust I’ll manage it well, so I end up stepping back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are causing you a lot of emotional pain or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s been pretty painful. I don’t always say it that way, but my mind just keeps going around and around—whether I’ll ever be hired again, whether parents think badly of me now… even small things, like if I make a mistake during story-time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children. It’s… hard to sit with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F45.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s the tension, mostly. My shoulders and neck are clenched all day, like I’m bracing for something at school that never actually ends. By the evening my jaw aches, and I can barely enjoy playing guitar.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens at school that makes you feel this tension?\n\nPatient: Not one thing, exactly. It’s more that my mind gets stuck… like, if I make a small mistake during story time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with the kids. Then it jumps to whether parents think badly of me, whether I’ve damaged my reputation, whether I’ll ever be hired again… things like that. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about making mistakes and your reputation feel like they're invading your mind, or are you able to intentionally think about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t really choose them, no. I try to steer away from it—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, just focus on the next sensible step—but… one worry sort of passes it to the next. Before I know it, I’ve lost half the afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worrying thoughts are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to put it. They feel like… my thoughts, I suppose. Not like a voice or something outside me. Just hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries mostly about your work with the kids or do they spread to other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It starts with the kids, usually… but it doesn’t stay there. It turns into whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, whether I’ll ever be hired again, whether retirement is slipping away. Even one small mistake during story time can somehow become, in my head, whether I should be trusted with children at all.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I keep to myself a bit more, maybe, but I haven’t thought of it as a relationship problem. I suppose I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your job or daily activities due to these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I’ve missed some application deadlines, and when I do get interviews, I don’t come across well. I get tangled up.\n\nI’ve also stopped volunteering for story-time unless someone else does the planning. So… yes, it’s getting in the way of me getting back into a classroom in any dependable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel short of breath, or experience palpitations, when these worries come up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that, no… not in any clear way. It’s more that I get caught up in the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your own health?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I’m 53, so things cross my mind now and then, but I wouldn’t say my health is what I’m usually worrying about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ve started avoiding things that would put me back in that role, I guess. Applications, interviews… even story-time, unless someone else handles the planning. It’s just hard to trust I’ll manage it well, so I end up stepping back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are causing you a lot of emotional pain or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s been pretty painful. I don’t always say it that way, but my mind just keeps going around and around—whether I’ll ever be hired again, whether parents think badly of me now… even small things, like if I make a mistake during story-time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children. It’s… hard to sit with.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things over and over, like checking or repeating actions, to try and calm your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I might look over things more than I need to, but I wouldn’t say I have to repeat actions to calm myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a fair way to put it. I mean… I suppose I’ve been a bit more withdrawn. I don’t really talk about it much, so I don’t know if it’s caused problems exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have damaged any of your close relationships?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d say damaged. Maybe I’ve been harder to reach lately, but I haven’t really asked anyone. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping due to these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly. I’ll lie there going over things—how I answered in interviews, bills, paperwork for licensing… and it just runs for hours. If I do fall asleep, I’m often up around four, already tense and thinking through what might go wrong that day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   {

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F45.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s the tension, mostly. My shoulders and neck are clenched all day, like I’m bracing for something at school that never actually ends. By the evening my jaw aches, and I can barely enjoy playing guitar.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens at school that makes you feel this tension?\n\nPatient: Not one thing, exactly. It’s more that my mind gets stuck… like, if I make a small mistake during story time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with the kids. Then it jumps to whether parents think badly of me, whether I’ve damaged my reputation, whether I’ll ever be hired again… things like that. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about making mistakes and your reputation feel like they're invading your mind, or are you able to intentionally think about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t really choose them, no. I try to steer away from it—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, just focus on the next sensible step—but… one worry sort of passes it to the next. Before I know it, I’ve lost half the afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worrying thoughts are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to put it. They feel like… my thoughts, I suppose. Not like a voice or something outside me. Just hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries mostly about your work with the kids or do they spread to other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It starts with the kids, usually… but it doesn’t stay there. It turns into whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, whether I’ll ever be hired again, whether retirement is slipping away. Even one small mistake during story time can somehow become, in my head, whether I should be trusted with children at all.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I keep to myself a bit more, maybe, but I haven’t thought of it as a relationship problem. I suppose I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your job or daily activities due to these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I’ve missed some application deadlines, and when I do get interviews, I don’t come across well. I get tangled up.\n\nI’ve also stopped volunteering for story-time unless someone else does the planning. So… yes, it’s getting in the way of me getting back into a classroom in any dependable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel short of breath, or experience palpitations, when these worries come up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that, no… not in any clear way. It’s more that I get caught up in the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your own health?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I’m 53, so things cross my mind now and then, but I wouldn’t say my health is what I’m usually worrying about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ve started avoiding things that would put me back in that role, I guess. Applications, interviews… even story-time, unless someone else handles the planning. It’s just hard to trust I’ll manage it well, so I end up stepping back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are causing you a lot of emotional pain or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s been pretty painful. I don’t always say it that way, but my mind just keeps going around and around—whether I’ll ever be hired again, whether parents think badly of me now… even small things, like if I make a mistake during story-time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children. It’s… hard to sit with.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things over and over, like checking or repeating actions, to try and calm your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I might look over things more than I need to, but I wouldn’t say I have to repeat actions to calm myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a fair way to put it. I try to redirect myself—make a list, pray, go pull weeds in the garden, focus on just the next thing I can do—but it doesn’t really stay put. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships due to your worries about work or reputation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I suppose I’ve been a bit more withdrawn. I don’t really talk about it much, so I don’t know if it’s caused problems exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have damaged any of your close relationships?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d say damaged. Maybe I’ve been harder to reach lately, but I haven’t really asked anyone. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping due to these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly. I’ll lie there going over things—how I answered in interviews, bills, paperwork for licensing… and it just runs for hours. If I do fall asleep, I’m often up around four, already tense and thinking through what might go wrong that day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any hobbies or activities outside of work and spending time with others that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guitar, mostly. I used to play in the evenings, but lately by then my shoulders and neck are so tight, and my jaw hurts from clenching, that I just… don’t really enjoy it. It feels like I’m bracing all day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t really notice that, no… not in any clear way. It’s more that I get caught up in the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your own health?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I’m 53, so things cross my mind now and then, but I wouldn’t say my health is what I’m usually worrying about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ve started avoiding things that would put me back in that role, I guess. Applications, interviews… even story-time, unless someone else handles the planning. It’s just hard to trust I’ll manage it well, so I end up stepping back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are causing you a lot of emotional pain or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s been pretty painful. I don’t always say it that way, but my mind just keeps going around and around—whether I’ll ever be hired again, whether parents think badly of me now… even small things, like if I make a mistake during story-time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children. It’s… hard to sit with.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things over and over, like checking or repeating actions, to try and calm your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I might look over things more than I need to, but I wouldn’t say I have to repeat actions to calm myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a fair way to put it. I try to redirect myself—make a list, pray, go pull weeds in the garden, focus on just the next thing I can do—but it doesn’t really stay put. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships due to your worries about work or reputation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I suppose I’ve been a bit more withdrawn. I don’t really talk about it much, so I don’t know if it’s caused problems exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have damaged any of your close relationships?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d say damaged. Maybe I’ve been harder to reach lately, but I haven’t really asked anyone. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping due to these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly. I’ll lie there going over things—how I answered in interviews, bills, paperwork for licensing… and it just runs for hours. If I do fall asleep, I’m often up around four, already tense and thinking through what might go wrong that day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any hobbies or activities outside of work and spending time with others that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guitar, mostly. I used to play in the evenings, but lately by then my shoulders and neck are so tight, and my jaw hurts from clenching, that I just… don’t really enjoy it. It feels like I’m bracing all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have impacted your ability to form or maintain close relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve been more… closed off, maybe. But I don’t know if it’s actually affected relationships in a clear way. I haven’t really talked to anyone about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s the tension, mostly. My shoulders and neck are clenched all day, like I’m bracing for something at school that never actually ends. By the evening my jaw aches, and I can barely enjoy playing guitar.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens at school that makes you feel this tension?\n\nPatient: Not one thing, exactly. It’s more that my mind gets stuck… like, if I make a small mistake during story time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with the kids. Then it jumps to whether parents think badly of me, whether I’ve damaged my reputation, whether I’ll ever be hired again… things like that. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about making mistakes and your reputation feel like they're invading your mind, or are you able to intentionally think about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t really choose them, no. I try to steer away from it—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, just focus on the next sensible step—but… one worry sort of passes it to the next. Before I know it, I’ve lost half the afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worrying thoughts are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to put it. They feel like… my thoughts, I suppose. Not like a voice or something outside me. Just hard to shut off.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries mostly about your work with the kids or do they spread to other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It starts with the kids, usually… but it doesn’t stay there. It turns into whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, whether I’ll ever be hired again, whether retirement is slipping away. Even one small mistake during story time can somehow become, in my head, whether I should be trusted with children at all.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I keep to myself a bit more, maybe, but I haven’t thought of it as a relationship problem. I suppose I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your job or daily activities due to these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I’ve missed some application deadlines, and when I do get interviews, I don’t come across well. I get tangled up.\n\nI’ve also stopped volunteering for story-time unless someone else does the planning. So… yes, it’s getting in the way of me getting back into a classroom in any dependable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel short of breath, or experience palpitations, when these worries come up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that, no… not in any clear way. It’s more that I get caught up in the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your own health?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I’m 53, so things cross my mind now and then, but I wouldn’t say my health is what I’m usually worrying about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ve started avoiding things that would put me back in that role, I guess. Applications, interviews… even story-time, unless someone else handles the planning. It’s just hard to trust I’ll manage it well, so I end up stepping back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are causing you a lot of emotional pain or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s been pretty painful. I don’t always say it that way, but my mind just keeps going around and around—whether I’ll ever be hired again, whether parents think badly of me now… even small things, like if I make a mistake during story-time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children. It’s… hard to sit with.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things over and over, like checking or repeating actions, to try and calm your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I might look over things more than I need to, but I wouldn’t say I have to repeat actions to calm myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a fair way to put it. I try to redirect myself—make a list, pray, go pull weeds in the garden, focus on just the next thing I can do—but it doesn’t really stay put. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships due to your worries about work or reputation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I suppose I’ve been a bit more withdrawn. I don’t really talk about it much, so I don’t know if it’s caused problems exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have damaged any of your close relationships?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d say damaged. Maybe I’ve been harder to reach lately, but I haven’t really asked anyone. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping due to these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly. I’ll lie there going over things—how I answered in interviews, bills, paperwork for licensing… and it just runs for hours. If I do fall asleep, I’m often up around four, already tense and thinking through what might go wrong that day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any hobbies or activities outside of work and spending time with others that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guitar, mostly. I used to play in the evenings, but lately by then my shoulders and neck are so tight, and my jaw hurts from clenching, that I just… don’t really enjoy it. It feels like I’m bracing all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have impacted your ability to form or maintain close relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve been more… closed off, maybe. But I don’t know if it’s actually affected relationships in a clear way. I haven’t really talked to anyone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach problems when you're worried?\n\nPatient: Mostly tension, I guess. My shoulders and neck are clenched pretty much all day… like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. So… yes, it’s getting in the way of me getting back into a classroom in any dependable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel short of breath, or experience palpitations, when these worries come up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that, no… not in any clear way. It’s more that I get caught up in the worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about your own health?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I’m 53, so things cross my mind now and then, but I wouldn’t say my health is what I’m usually worrying about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ve started avoiding things that would put me back in that role, I guess. Applications, interviews… even story-time, unless someone else handles the planning. It’s just hard to trust I’ll manage it well, so I end up stepping back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are causing you a lot of emotional pain or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s been pretty painful. I don’t always say it that way, but my mind just keeps going around and around—whether I’ll ever be hired again, whether parents think badly of me now… even small things, like if I make a mistake during story-time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children. It’s… hard to sit with.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things over and over, like checking or repeating actions, to try and calm your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. I might look over things more than I need to, but I wouldn’t say I have to repeat actions to calm myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a fair way to put it. I try to redirect myself—make a list, pray, go pull weeds in the garden, focus on just the next thing I can do—but it doesn’t really stay put. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships due to your worries about work or reputation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I suppose I’ve been a bit more withdrawn. I don’t really talk about it much, so I don’t know if it’s caused problems exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have damaged any of your close relationships?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d say damaged. Maybe I’ve been harder to reach lately, but I haven’t really asked anyone. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping due to these worries?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly. I’ll lie there going over things—how I answered in interviews, bills, paperwork for licensing… and it just runs for hours. If I do fall asleep, I’m often up around four, already tense and thinking through what might go wrong that day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any hobbies or activities outside of work and spending time with others that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guitar, mostly. I used to play in the evenings, but lately by then my shoulders and neck are so tight, and my jaw hurts from clenching, that I just… don’t really enjoy it. It feels like I’m bracing all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worries have impacted your ability to form or maintain close relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve been more… closed off, maybe. But I don’t know if it’s actually affected relationships in a clear way. I haven’t really talked to anyone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach problems when you're worried?\n\nPatient: Mostly tension, I guess. My shoulders and neck are clenched pretty much all day… like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. By evening my jaw aches enough that even playing guitar isn’t really enjoyable.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries about work or reputation caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s gotten in the way more than I like to admit. I’ve missed application deadlines, and when I do get interviews I kind of stumble through them. I’ve also stopped volunteering for story-time unless someone else is handling the planning. So… it’s making it hard to get back into the classroom in any dependable way."
